Remark 5.3 (Alternative derivation via mutual information). Let us give an alternative and more
conceptual derivation of Eq. (36). Let b ∼ Ber( 12 ) and e ∼ Ber(𝜂) be independent random bits. Set
y B b ⊕ e ∼ Ber( 12 ). Since b ↦−→ b ⊕ e ↦−→ 𝜎b⊕e
                                              PSC
                                                   is a Markov chain, using the mutual information
chain rule, the identity 𝜎b⊕e = 𝑋 𝜎e 𝑋 , the observation in Footnote 9, and the Holevo identity
                           PSC     b  PSC  b

(Eq. (13)), notice that

                         𝐼(y; 𝜎yPSC ) = 𝐼(b, e; 𝜎b⊕e
                                                 PSC
                                                     ) = 𝐼(b; 𝜎b⊕e
                                                               PSC
                                                                   ) + 𝐼(e; 𝜎b⊕e
                                                                             PSC
                                                                                 | b)
                                                     = 𝜒MQC (𝑟, 𝜂) + 𝐼(e; 𝜎ePSC ).

Thus, we find that
                                  𝜒MQC (𝑟, 𝜂) = 𝐼(y; 𝜎yPSC ) − 𝐼(e; 𝜎ePSC ).
Because the bit y remains uniform for every 𝜂, the first term is always 𝐼(y; 𝜎yPSC ) = 𝑠((1 − 2𝑟)2 ).
Thus, the dependence on 𝜂 lies in the second term, which equals 𝐼(e; 𝜎ePSC ) = 𝑠(4𝜂(1 − 𝜂)(1 − 2𝑟)2 ).
Thus, mixing starts with the information carried by the underlying PSC and subtracts exactly the
information that its output state 𝜎y retains about the discarded flip e once b is known.            ⊳

Remark 5.4 (Holevo information and PGM error data processing). As was noted in Section 1.3, as 𝜂
increases along the interval [0, 12 ], we expect the Holevo information to decay while the PGM error
to grow. Indeed, by inspecting Eqs. (34) and (36), one can numerically confirm that this is indeed
the case here.                                                                                     ⊳

5.2   Masked mixed-qubit channel (2MQC)

We next apply the masking operation of Section 2.5 to the MQC pair. Fix 𝑟, 𝜂, 𝛼 ∈ [0, 12 ]. Throughout,

Remark 5.5 (2MQC includes both mPSC and MQC). Almost by construction, the mPSC and MQC
are both special cases of the 2MQC. Indeed, at 𝜂 = 0, the 𝑋-Pauli noise is no longer there, and so
2MQC(𝑟, 0, 𝛼) is exactly mPSC(𝑟, 𝛼). Moreover, when 𝛼 = 12 , since masking with a uniform prior
restores the original channel (Remark 2.2), we see that 2MQC(𝑟, 𝜂, 12 ) is equivalent to MQC(𝑟, 𝜂). ⊳

The pretty good criterion (Theorem 1) applies whenever 𝐸(𝑢, 𝑣, 𝐴) < 𝛿. Fix a pair (𝑢, 𝑣) with

affine in 𝐴, whereas (44) decreases with 𝐴. As in the MQC calculation (Section 5.1.3), the lowest

solution to the equation 𝐸(𝑢, 𝑣, 𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣)) = 𝛿. Solving for 𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) in this equation, we find that

                                                 (𝑢 2 − 𝑣 2 )2 + 2𝛿𝑢 2 (1 + 𝑢)
                                  𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) =                                    .                                 (45)
                                                  𝑢 2 (1 − 𝑣 2 ) + 𝑣 2 (𝑢 + 𝑣 2 )

Since we must have 𝐴 ∈ [0, 1], we therefore see that the optimal value for 𝐴 must be

                                                (𝑢 2 − 𝑣 2 )2 + 2𝛿𝑢 2 (1 + 𝑢)

                             𝐴 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) B min 1, 2                             .

we know that 𝐴 = (𝑢 2 − 𝑣 2 )/(1 − 𝑣 2 ) is the smallest possible value of 𝐴. In such a case, the PGM bit

                                               𝑢2 − 𝑣2     𝑣 2 (𝑢 2 − 𝑣 2 )

                                            𝑣 2 (𝑢 2 − 𝑣 2 ) < 2𝛿𝑢 2 (1 − 𝑣 2 ).

Now, we take stock of everything and conclude our rate bound. By applying the pretty good

where we define 𝐴 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) B min 1, 𝑢 2 (1−𝑣 2 )+𝑣 2 (𝑢+𝑣 2 ) .


6    Comparing the rate bounds
In Section 4, we recovered all four previously known rate upper bounds—Plotkin, Elias–Bassalygo,
the first and second MRRW bounds. In Section 5, we produced two new rate upper bounds from
the newly introduced cq channels MQC and 2MQC, which are stated in Corollaries 23 and 24. In
this section, we analytically and numerically compare the rate bounds of MQC and 2MQC with the
first and second MRRW bounds, respectively, and establish Theorems 2 and 3. For the reader’s
convenience, we recap the parameters and formulas of all six channels in Table 1 below.

the Johnson radius, while 𝑠(𝐷) B ℎ((1 − 1 − 𝐷)/2) is the von Neumann entropy of a qubit with
determinant 𝐷/4. The variables 𝑢, 𝑣, 𝐴 are functions of 𝑟, 𝜂, 𝛼 and are specified at Sections 4.4.3,
5.1.3 and 5.2.3. Here, the functions 𝑒 and 𝐸 are defined at Eqs. (37) and (42), respectively.

6.1    MQC improvement over the first MRRW bound
In this subsection, we prove that slightly deviating away from PSC by considering a small 𝑋-Pauli
noise in the MQC will make the MQC rate bound in Corollary 23 strictly smaller than the first
MRRW bound. This is stated in Proposition 25. For ease of exposition, we adopt the shorthand

                                      𝐹𝛿 (𝑢) B 𝑠(𝑢 2 ) − 𝑠 (𝑢(𝑢 − (1 − 2𝛿))) ,                                 (47)

for the second objective in (38), where 𝑢 ranges over [1 − 2𝛿, 1]. Note that by Remark 5.1, we know
that
                                 𝑅 MQC (𝛿) ⩽ min{𝑅 MRRW (𝛿), 𝑅EB (𝛿)}.
Now, we proceed to show that this is indeed strict for the first MRRW bound.25 For the reader’s
convenience, Fig. 1 provides a visual illustration of the argument used in the proof of the next
proposition.
Proposition 25 (Strict improvement over the first MRRW expression). For every 𝛿 ∈ (0, 12 ), we have
the strict inequality
                                    𝑅 MQC (𝛿) < 𝑅MRRW (𝛿).

Proof. By Remark 5.1 and Eqs. (38) and (47), notice that 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿) = 𝑅MRRW (𝛿). Now, fix 𝛿 and
choose a perturbation 𝜁 → 0+ of 1 − 2𝛿. Write the difference

           𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁) − 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿) = 𝑠((1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)2 ) − 𝑠((1 − 2𝛿)2 ) − 𝑠 ((1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)𝜁) .
                                                                                     

Now, for 𝐷 → 0+ , the definition of 𝑠 given at (21) gives
                                                     𝐷    1
                                           𝑠(𝐷) =      log + 𝑂(𝐷).
                                                     4    𝐷
Since (1 − 2𝛿)2 ∈ (0, 1), the mean value theorem thus gives us

                                     𝑠((1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)2 ) − 𝑠((1 − 2𝛿)2 ) = 𝑂(𝜁).
  25 One can similarly show that for the Elias–Bassalygo bound, the MQC bound strictly improves upon it, but only

when 𝛿 ≳ 0.07441947.


                                                          37
For the second term, which is where the primary gain in improved rate bounds stems from, its
argument tends to zero, so the preceding expansion gives

                                        (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)𝜁           1
                     𝑠((1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)𝜁) =               log               + 𝑂(𝜁)
                                              4           (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁)𝜁
                                        1 − 2𝛿      1
                                      =        𝜁 log + 𝑂(𝜁).
                                           4        𝜁
Therefore,
                        𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁) − 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿)    1 − 2𝛿    1
                                                      =−        log + 𝑂(1),
                                      𝜁                     4      𝜁
which tends to −∞ as 𝜁 → 0+ , as 1 − 2𝛿 > 0. Hence 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁) < 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿) for all sufficiently
small 𝜁 > 0. Thus, we conclude that

                       𝑅 MQC (𝛿) ⩽ 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿 + 𝜁) < 𝐹𝛿 (1 − 2𝛿) = 𝑅 MRRW (𝛿).                     □

   Now, we have established everything we need to prove Theorem 2, which we now show.

Proof of Theorem 2. The MQC rate upper bound in Corollary 23 gives 𝑅 2 (𝛿) ⩽ 𝑅MQC (𝛿), while
Proposition 25 gives 𝑅 MQC (𝛿) < 𝑅MRRW (𝛿) for every 𝛿 ∈ (0, 12 ). Combining the two inequalities
proves the theorem.                                                                             □

6.3   Comparing with the second MRRW bound
In this subsection, we prove the strict 2MQC improvement over the second MRRW bound
(Proposition 26) and complete the proof of Theorem 3. For ease of exposition, we adopt the
shorthand
                            𝐻𝛿 (𝑢) B 1 + 𝑠(𝑢 2 ) − 𝑠(𝑢 2 + 2𝛿𝑢 + 2𝛿),                  (48)
for the objective in (31), where 𝑢 ranges over [0, 1 − 2𝛿]. By Remark 5.5, the 2MQC contains the

                                                       n                                 o

                                  𝑅 2MQC (𝛿) ⩽ min         𝑅 MQC (𝛿), 𝑅MRRW (𝛿)              .

Proposition 26 (Strict improvement over the second MRRW bound). For every 𝛿 ∈ (0, 12 ), we have the
strict inequality
                                                  (2)
                                     𝑅2MQC (𝛿) < 𝑅MRRW (𝛿).

                            (2)
Proof. Suppose first that 𝑅MRRW (𝛿) = 𝑅MRRW (𝛿). By Remark 5.5 and Proposition 25, we have that
                                                                                   (2)
                           𝑅 2MQC (𝛿) ⩽ 𝑅 MQC (𝛿) < 𝑅MRRW (𝛿) = 𝑅MRRW (𝛿).
                                                 (2)
For the remainder of the proof, assume 𝑅 MRRW (𝛿) < 𝑅MRRW (𝛿). By (48) and the variational formula
of the second MRRW bound (31), we have
                                           (2)

The continuous function 𝐻𝛿 attains its minimum on [0, 1 − 2𝛿]. The right endpoint is not a minimizer
because
                                                           (2)

The left endpoint is not a minimizer either. Indeed, 𝑠(𝑢 2 ) = 𝑂(𝑢 2 log(1/𝑢)) has vanishing one-sided
derivative at 𝑢 = 0, and hence
                                      𝐻𝛿′ (0+ ) = −2𝛿𝑠 ′ (2𝛿) < 0.
Thus, we can fix a minimizer 𝑢∗ ∈ (0, 1 − 2𝛿).
     Now, observe that the mPSC endpoint is when 𝑣 = 0. Indeed, by (43), we have 𝑣 = 0 if and only
if 𝜂 = 0, which recovers mPSC by Remark 5.5. Now, define the shorthand

                              𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) B 1 + 𝑠(𝑢 2 ) − 𝑠 (𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣)) − 𝑠(𝑣 2 ),                       (49)

where 𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢, 𝑣) is as defined in Eq. (45). Note that when 𝑣 = 0, we have that 𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 0) = 𝑢∗2 + 2𝛿𝑢∗ + 2𝛿.
Thus
                                                                 (2)

as expected by Remark 5.5.
    We will now perturb 𝑣 → 0+ slightly away from 0 on the positive side while keeping the PGM

Thus, for all sufficiently small 𝑣 > 0, we have that 𝐴 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣) = 𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣). Consequently, by Eq. (46),
we have
                                         𝑅2MQC (𝛿) ⩽ 𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣)                                           (51)
for 𝑣 → 0+ . Now, write the difference

By the mean value theorem, we have that

                                         𝑠 (𝜆 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣)) − 𝑠(𝜆0 ) = 𝑂(𝑣 2 ).

For the second term, the expansion used in the proof of Proposition 25 gives

                                                       𝑣2    1
                                           𝑠(𝑣 2 ) =      log + 𝑂(𝑣 2 ).
                                                       2     𝑣
Therefore,
                           𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣) − 𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 0)    1   1
                                                       = − log + 𝑂(1),
                                       𝑣 2                2   𝑣
which tends to −∞ as 𝑣 → 0 . Hence
                          +


                                              𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣) < 𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 0)                                   (52)

for all sufficiently small 𝑣 > 0. Combining Eqs. (50) to (52), we therefore conclude that
                                                                              (2)
                             𝑅 2MQC (𝛿) ⩽ 𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 𝑣) < 𝐺 𝛿 (𝑢∗ , 0) = 𝑅 MRRW (𝛿).                           □

    With this proposition at hand, we can now establish Theorem 3.

Proof of Theorem 3. The theorem follows by combining the 2MQC rate upper bound 𝑅2 (𝛿) ⩽
                                                                             (2)
𝑅 2MQC (𝛿) (Corollary 24) together with the strict inequality 𝑅 2MQC (𝛿) < 𝑅 MRRW (𝛿) (Proposition 26).
                                                                                                              □

7    Extending to 𝑞-ary codes
In this section, we sketch how one might extend our framework to 𝑞-ary codes. Fix an integer 𝑞 ⩾ 2,
write [𝑞] B {0, . . . , 𝑞 − 1}, and let 𝐴 𝑞 (𝑛, 𝑑) be the largest cardinality of a code in [𝑞]𝑛 with minimum
distance at least 𝑑. We normalize rates in base 𝑞 and write
                                                         1
                                     𝑅 𝑞 (𝛿) B lim sup     log𝑞 𝐴 𝑞 (𝑛, ⌈𝛿𝑛⌉).
                                                𝑛→∞      𝑛

For 𝛿 ⩾ 1 − 1𝑞 , the 𝑞-ary Plotkin bound shows that 𝑅 𝑞 (𝛿) = 0. Thus, the nontrivial interval in the
𝑞-ary setting is 𝛿 ∈ (0, 1 − 1𝑞 ). Since the pretty good criterion (Theorem 1) is stated for binary codes,
we first extend its output-symmetry hypothesis to the cyclic 𝑞-ary alphabet.
Definition 7.1 (𝑞-ary output-symmetry). For a positive integer 𝑑, a 𝑞-ary-input cq channel Φ𝜎 : [𝑞] →
D(C𝑑 ), given by Φ𝜎 (𝑥) = 𝜎𝑥 , is 𝑞-ary output-symmetric if there is a unitary 𝑈 such that

                                                𝑈 𝜎𝑥 𝑈 † = 𝜎𝑥+1

for every 𝑥 ∈ [𝑞], where subscripts and addition are taken modulo 𝑞. Iterating this identity gives
𝑈 𝑎 𝜎𝑥 (𝑈 † )𝑎 = 𝜎𝑥+𝑎 for all 𝑎, 𝑥 ∈ [𝑞].

    With this terminology in place, the following proposition records the precise 𝑞-ary extension.
Proposition 27 (𝑞-ary pretty good criterion). Let Φ𝜎 : 𝑥 ↦→ 𝜎𝑥 be a 𝑞-ary output-symmetric cq channel.

minimum distance at least 𝛿𝑛 satisfies

Each 𝑞-MC output has spectrum 𝜆+ (𝑝, 𝑣), 𝜆− (𝑝, 𝑣), and ℓ 𝑞 (𝑝, 𝑣) with multiplicity 𝑞 − 2, while the
average state has spectrum 1 − 𝑔 𝑞 (𝑣) and 𝑔 𝑞 (𝑣)/(𝑞 − 1) with multiplicity 𝑞 − 1. These spectra give
the normalized Holevo information Ξ𝑞 (𝑝, 𝑣), and the uniform-input PGM symbol error is exactly 𝑝.
   The unitary that sends |𝑥⟩ to |𝑥 + 1⟩ (and fixes |?⟩ for the 𝑞-EC) shows that all four channels are

                                                                                                       𝑞,cq (𝛿)



    The first three rows recover the 𝑞-ary Plotkin bound [Plo60], the 𝑞-ary Elias–Bassalygo
bound [Eli55, Bas65], and the first Delsarte–Levenshtein linear-programming bound [Del73, Lev95],
respectively. The general 𝑞-ary linear-programming framework originates with Delsarte [Del72,
Del73], was developed systematically by Levenshtein [Lev95] and Delsarte and Levenshtein [DL98],
and admits more recent explicit dual constructions [CDA25]. Kaipa’s anticode-based hybrid, using
the optimal-anticode theorem of Ahlswede and Khachatrian, improves both the 𝑞-ary Elias and
Plotkin bounds [AK98, Kai18]. At 𝑞 = 2, the 𝑞-PSC row is the first MRRW bound [MRRW77], while
the 𝑞-MC row becomes the binary MQC bound.
    The 𝑞-MC supplies the desired 𝑞-ary output-symmetric fixed-error path from 𝑞-SC(𝐽𝑞 (𝑝)) to
𝑞-PSC(𝑝). We do not claim here that it improves the best known 𝑞-ary bounds when 𝑞 > 2.
Its optimized curve should first be compared with Aaltonen’s nonbinary linear-programming
bound [Aal90], the shortening and straight-line bound of Laihonen and Litsyn [LL98], and the
product-association-scheme bounds of Ben-Haim and Litsyn [BHL07]. Searching beyond the 𝑞-MC
family is represented by the last row of the table.


8   PSC rate bounds for LDPC codes
This section proves Theorem 4. It is primarily intended as a proof of concept showing how the
pretty good criterion (Theorem 1) can incorporate structure specific to a code family and thereby
improve a specialized rate–distance bound. For simplicity, we specialize throughout to parity
checks of weight at most 3.
   We begin with thep benchmark bound of Shangguan and Yang [SY26] in the notation of this
paper. Set 𝑟 𝛿 B 2 − 𝛿(1 − 𝛿). The 𝑤 = 3 bound in their Theorem 1.5, with their base-four entropy
                 1

expanded into base-two logarithms, is
                                   (
                                       3,                                     𝑟 𝛿 ⩾ 41 ,
                                       2
                      𝐵3SY (𝛿) B                                                                                             (56)
                                       −𝑟 𝛿 log 𝑟 𝛿 − 1−3𝑟 𝛿
                                                        3 log(1 − 3𝑟 𝛿 ),     0 < 𝑟 𝛿 < 41 ,

extended continuously by 𝐵3SY ( 12 ) B 0. Throughout 25 ⩽ 𝛿 < 12 , this is the 𝑤 = 3 bound obtained
in [SY26]. In proving Theorem 4, we will compare the rate bound obtained from the PSC with this

                                                       48
benchmark for 𝛿 ∈ [ 16 , 21 ).
    Recall the two exact optimality statements for PGM decoding over PSC from Section 1.1. Namely,
for binary linear codes, the PGM achieves optimal error at both the block and bit levels. In this
section, we will rely on PGM’s bitwise optimality, stated below.

Fact 28 (Bitwise PGM optimality for linear codes — [PR25, Proposition 5.1]). Let 𝒞 ⩽ F2𝑛 be linear and
                                                      PSC(𝑝)
𝑝 ∈ (0, 12 ). For the uniform ensemble {(|𝒞 |−1 , 𝜎𝑐          ) : 𝑐 ∈ 𝒞 }, let ĉ be the outcome of the PGM. For any
coordinate 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑛}, the 𝑖’th outcome bit ĉ𝑖 is the Helstrom-optimal measurement [Hel69] for decoding
c𝑖 . In particular, the error of any explicit decoder for c𝑖 upper-bounds the bitwise error 𝑝 e,𝑖 of the block PGM.

   The explicit decoder we use will apply the equal-prior Helstrom measurement directly to
the PSC outputs indexed by one weight-3 parity check containing the desired bit. Define the
weight-three error function
                                                                           p
                                          1 + 4𝑝(1 − 𝑝) − (1 − 2𝑝) 1 + 12𝑝(1 − 𝑝)
                           𝑒3LDPC (𝑝) B                                                           .            (57)
                                                                     4

   We next bound this Helstrom error and then invoke the bitwise optimality of the code PGM to
bound 𝑝e,𝑖 from above.

Lemma 29 (One-check PSC decoder). Let 𝑝 ∈ (0, 12 ). Let 𝒞 ⩽ F2𝑛 have minimum distance greater than
one and let 𝒞 ⊥ be generated by checks of weight at most 3. If a uniformly random c ∈ 𝒞 is encoded into the
               PSC(𝑝)
product state 𝜎c      , then for every 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑛}, the bitwise coarse-graining of the block PGM has error

                                                 𝑝 e,𝑖 ⩽ 𝑒3LDPC (𝑝) < 𝑝.

Proof. Fix 𝑖 ∈ {1, . . . , 𝑛}. Because 𝒞 ⊥ is generated by checks of weight at most 3, some such
generating check contains 𝑖; otherwise, the weight-one vector supported at 𝑖 would lie in 𝒞 , contrary
to the minimum-distance assumption. Choose a minimum-weight ℎ ∈ 𝒞 ⊥ with ℎ 𝑖 = 1. If wt(ℎ) = 1,
the check fixes c𝑖 = 0, so the bitwise error is zero. Suppose henceforth that wt(ℎ) ∈ {2, 3}. The
minimality of ℎ implies that the weight-one vector supported at 𝑖 is not in 𝒞 ⊥ ; hence 𝑐 ↦→ 𝑐 𝑖 is a
nonzero linear functional on 𝒞 , and c𝑖 is uniform.
     By Fact 28, the error of any decoder for c𝑖 upper-bounds 𝑝 e,𝑖 . Suppose first that ℎ has support
{𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘}. Minimality implies that the projection of 𝒞 onto these coordinates is the full even-parity
code {000, 011, 101, 110}. Indeed, any additional linear relation on the projection, or its sum with ℎ,
would give a check of weight at most two containing 𝑖. Since c is uniform on 𝒞 , its projection is
uniform as well. Thus, conditioned on c𝑖 = 𝑏, the three outputs have states
                                    PSC(𝑝)        PSC(𝑝)                    PSC(𝑝)       PSC(𝑝)

A    Proofs for the preliminaries
In this appendix, for the sake of completeness, we present the non-immediate proofs deferred from
Section 2, particularly the PGM error data processing inequality (Lemma 10) and the cq channel
coding strong converse (Theorem 7), as those are the two crucial inequalities needed in the proof of
the pretty good criterion (Theorem 1).
   To this end, we recall some necessary quantum notions and tools. Building on the notion of a
density matrix, a 𝑑 × 𝑑 matrix is said to be a subnormalized state if it is a positive semidefinite matrix
whose trace is at most 1. For any finite-dimensional Hilbert space 𝑉, let ℳ(𝑉) denote the vector
space of all Hermitian matrices over 𝑉.
    Next, we recall some standard matrix analysis tools needed in the proof of the cq channel
coding strong converse in Appendix A.4. We denote the tracepnorm of a 𝑑 × 𝑑 complex-valued
                         √
matrix 𝑋 by p ∥𝑋∥1 B Tr 𝑋 † 𝑋, the Frobenius norm by ∥𝑋∥2 B Tr(𝑋 † 𝑋), and the spectral norm
by ∥𝑋∥∞ B 𝜆max (𝑋 † 𝑋). Throughout, matrix inequalities 𝐴 ⪯ 𝐵 between Hermitian matrices
mean that 𝐵 − 𝐴 is positive semidefinite. Note that for diagonal density matrices, i.e., classical
probability distributions, the quantity 12 ∥𝜏−𝜏′ ∥1 is the total-variation distance between the probability
distributions 𝜏 and 𝜏′ .
   We now recall a standard inequality that is needed for the proof of the cq channel coding strong
converse (Appendix A.4), which is the gentle measurement lemma, stated in [Wil21, Lemma 9.4.2
and Exercise 9.4.1]. The gentle measurement lemma controls how much a test that passes with high
probability disturbs such a state. Classically, conditioning on an event of probability at least 1 − 𝜀
                                                                                                  √
moves a distribution by 𝑂(𝜀) in total variation. In the quantum case, that estimate becomes 𝑂( 𝜀).

Lemma 31 (Gentle measurement lemma). If 𝐴 is a subnormalized 𝑑 × 𝑑 state and 𝑃 is a projection 𝑑 × 𝑑
matrix, then                                   p
                              ∥𝐴 − 𝑃𝐴𝑃∥1 ⩽ 2 Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)].

Proof. Since 𝐼 − 𝑃 and 𝑃 are both projection matrices, by the triangle inequality, the matrix Cauchy–
Schwarz inequality [Bha97, Corollary IV.2.6], and the inequalities Tr(𝐴) ⩽ 1 and max{Tr(𝑃𝐴), Tr((𝐼 −



                                                    63
𝑃)𝐴)} ⩽ Tr(𝐴) (as 𝐴 is a subnormalized state), we find that

                  ∥𝐴 − 𝑃𝐴𝑃∥1 = ∥(𝐼 − 𝑃)𝐴 + 𝑃𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)∥1
                                   ⩽ ∥(𝐼 − 𝑃)𝐴∥1 + ∥𝑃𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)∥1
                                   ⩽ ∥(𝐼 − 𝑃)𝐴1/2 ∥2 · ∥𝐴1/2 ∥2 + ∥𝑃𝐴1/2 ∥2 · ∥𝐴1/2 (𝐼 − 𝑃)∥2
                                     p                 p          p           p
                                   =     Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)] · Tr(𝐴) + Tr(𝑃𝐴) · Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)]
                                       p                       p
                                   ⩽       Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)] · 1 + 1 · Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)]
                                        p
                                   = 2 Tr[𝐴(𝐼 − 𝑃)].                                                     □

A.1    Petz recovery and its structural properties
In this subsection, we develop the Petz recovery map [Pet84, Pet86, Pet88, Ren22], a generalization of
the PGM, and prove its structural properties (Fact 32), en route to proving the PGM data-processing
lemma (Lemma 10) in Appendix A.2. A Petz map reverses an arbitrary quantum channel relative
to a chosen reference state. When the channel is a cq preparation channel and the reference is its
classical prior, the Petz map reduces to the PGM [BK02]. This identification explains the connection
with the PGM.
    As a brief digression, recent work uses Petz maps and their variants to quantify approximate
recovery in strengthened data-processing inequalities [STH16, JRS+ 18], to construct explicit decoders
for quantum communication [BDL16], to reconstruct holographic entanglement wedges [CPS20],
and to design quantum algorithms for Petz channels and PGMs [GLM+ 22].
   Returning to the subject matter, we now formally introduce the Petz map.

Definition A.1 (Petz recovery map). Let Φ : C𝑑𝐴 ×𝑑𝐴 → C𝑑𝐵 ×𝑑𝐵 be a quantum channel, and let 𝜋 ∈ D(C𝑑𝐴 )
be a reference state. Let 𝜈 B Φ(𝜋) ∈ D(C𝑑𝐵 ). The Petz recovery map of Φ relative to 𝜋 is the linear map
  ♯
Φ𝜋 : C𝑑𝐵 ×𝑑𝐵 → C𝑑𝐴 ×𝑑𝐴 defined as
                                                                     
                                        ♯
                                       Φ𝜋 (𝑌) B 𝜋1/2 Φ∗ 𝜈−1/2𝑌𝜈−1/2 𝜋1/2 ,

where Φ∗ is determined by the identity Tr[𝑋 † Φ∗ (𝑌)] = Tr[Φ(𝑋)†𝑌], and inverse powers act on the indicated
supports. Moreover, for 𝐴1 , 𝐴2 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜋)), define the weighted inner product
                                                                         
                                       ⟨𝐴1 , 𝐴2 ⟩𝜋 B Tr 𝐴†1 𝜋−1/2 𝐴2 𝜋−1/2 .

Since ⟨𝐴, 𝐴⟩𝜋 = ∥𝜋−1/4 𝐴𝜋−1/4 ∥22 , this weighted form is therefore positive definite over ℳ(supp(𝜋)).

Remark A.1 (Petz in the classical case). Let 𝑊(𝑦 | 𝑥) be any classical channel with input and© output
alphabets 𝒳 and 𝒴 , let 𝜋(𝑥) be an input distribution, and let 𝜈(𝑦) B 𝑥∈𝒳 𝜋(𝑥)𝑊(𝑦 | 𝑥) be the
                                                                            Í
distribution of 𝜋 when passed through 𝑊. For every output 𝑦 with 𝜈(𝑦) > 0, the Petz recovery map
is the Bayes reverse channel

                               ♯                𝜋(𝑥)𝑊(𝑦 | 𝑥)
                            𝑊𝜋 (𝑥 | 𝑦) B                     = Pr[x = 𝑥 | y = 𝑦].
                                                    𝜈(𝑦)
Thus, Petz recovery is the quantum form of reversing a channel by Bayes’ rule relative to a fixed
prior.                                                                                          ⊳

                                                         64
    The proof of PGM data processing in Appendix A.2 uses the following three structural properties
of this recovery map.

Fact 32 (Petz adjointness, functoriality, and contraction). Following the notation given in Definition A.1,
                                                       ♯
when restricted to ℳ(supp(𝜈)), the Petz recovery map Φ𝜋 is a quantum channel with output density matrices
residing in ℳ(supp(𝜋)). Moreover, the following three properties hold:

  (i) (Weighted adjointness). For all 𝐴 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜋)) and 𝐵 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜈)), we have the identity
                                                                               D           E
                                                                                       ♯
                                                       ⟨Φ(𝐴), 𝐵⟩𝜈 = 𝐴, Φ𝜋 (𝐵)                      .
                                                                                               𝜋


 (ii) (Contravariant functoriality). For any quantum channel Ψ : C𝑑𝐵 ×𝑑𝐵 → C𝑑𝐶 ×𝑑𝐶 , we have the
      composition identity
                                                ♯    ♯    ♯
                                         (Ψ ◦ Φ)𝜋 = Φ𝜋 ◦ Ψ𝜈 .
                                                                                                               ♯
 (iii) (Petz–Gram contraction). For all 𝐴1 , 𝐴2 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜋)), the quantum channel 𝐾 B Φ𝜋 ◦ Φ satisfies

                                                  ⟨𝐴1 , 𝐾(𝐴2 )⟩𝜋 = ⟨Φ(𝐴1 ), Φ(𝐴2 )⟩𝜈 .

      In particular, for any 𝐴 ∈ C𝑑𝐴 ×𝑑𝐴 , we have

                                                           0 ⩽ ⟨𝐴, 𝐾(𝐴)⟩𝜋 ⩽ ⟨𝐴, 𝐴⟩𝜋 .                              (62)

Proof of Fact 32. For every 𝐵 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜈)), from Definition 2.1, we have that 𝐵Π𝜈 = 𝐵. Combining

and Φ∗ are all completely positive,26 the closure of completely positive maps under composition
                      ♯                                 ♯
therefore shows that Φ𝜋 is completely positive. Thus, Φ𝜋 is a quantum channel when restricted to

   For Fact 32(ii), define 𝜇 B Ψ(𝜈). For 𝐶 ∈ D(supp(𝜇)), applying the preceding adjointness

which proves the functoriality formula by uniqueness of the adjoint. Note that the adjoint map, in

    It remains to prove Fact 32(iii). To this end, we first show that 𝐾 is positive semidefinite with
respect to the 𝜋-weighted inner product ⟨·, ·⟩𝜋 from Definition A.1, which enables us to invoke the
(finite-dimensional) spectral theorem. Indeed, weighted adjointness (i) tells us that

                                        ⟨𝐴1 , 𝐾(𝐴2 )⟩𝜋 = ⟨Φ(𝐴1 ), Φ(𝐴2 )⟩𝜈 .

In particular, ⟨𝐴, 𝐾(𝐴)⟩𝜋 = ⟨Φ(𝐴), Φ(𝐴)⟩𝜈 ⩾ 0. Moreover, conjugate symmetry gives us

                ⟨𝐾(𝐴), 𝐵⟩𝜋 = ⟨𝐵, 𝐾(𝐴)⟩𝜋 = ⟨Φ(𝐵), Φ(𝐴)⟩𝜈 = ⟨Φ(𝐴), Φ(𝐵)⟩𝜈 = ⟨𝐴, 𝐾(𝐵)⟩𝜋 .

Thus, 𝐾 is positive semidefinite with respect to the 𝜋-weighted inner product. By the spectral
theorem with respect to ⟨·, ·⟩𝜋 , we can find a weighted-eigenbasis {𝐸 𝑗 }𝑚
                                                                          𝑗=1
                                                                              satisfying 𝐸 𝑗 , 𝐸 𝑗 𝜋 = 1
and 𝐾(𝐸 𝑗 ) = 𝜆 𝑗 𝐸 𝑗 . Positivity gives 𝜆 𝑗 = 𝐸 𝑗 , 𝐾(𝐸 𝑗 ) 𝜋 ⩾ 0.
    We next show that every eigenvalue of 𝐾 lies in [0, 1]. Indeed, as a composition of support-
restricted quantum channels, 𝐾 is a quantum channel from ℳ(supp(𝜋)) to itself. Let 𝐾 ∗ de-
note its adjoint with respect to the trace inner product on ℳ(supp(𝜋)), defined by the identity
Tr[𝐴†1 𝐾 ∗ (𝐴2 )] = Tr[𝐾(𝐴1 )† 𝐴2 ]. Now, notice that the trace preservation of 𝐾 implies that (in fact,
equivalent) 𝐾 ∗ is unital, i.e., 𝐾 ∗ (Π𝜋 ) = Π𝜋 . Indeed, for every 𝐴 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜋)),

                    Tr 𝐴† 𝐾 ∗ (Π𝜋 ) = Tr 𝐾(𝐴)† Π𝜋 = Tr(𝐾(𝐴)) = Tr(𝐴) = Tr(𝐴† Π𝜋 ).
                                                      


The nondegeneracy of the trace inner product over ℳ(supp(𝜋)) thus implies that 𝐾 ∗ (Π𝜋 ) = Π𝜋 .
Since 𝐾 is a quantum channel over ℳ(supp(𝜋)), by the Stinespring representation [NC10, Chapter
8], there exist a positive integer 𝑘 and an isometry 𝑉 : supp(𝜋) → supp(𝜋) ⊗ C 𝑘 such that we
can write 𝐾(𝐴) = Tr𝐸 (𝑉 𝐴𝑉 † ), where Tr𝐸 : ℳ(supp(𝜋)) ⊗ ℳ(C 𝑘 ) → ℳ(supp(𝜋)) is the partial
trace map, i.e., the map 𝐴 ⊗ 𝑀 ↦→ 𝐴 Tr(𝑀). By uniqueness of adjoints, we can therefore write
𝐾 ∗ (𝑋) = 𝑉 † (𝑋 ⊗ 𝐼 𝑘 )𝑉 for all 𝑋 ∈ ℳ(supp(𝜋)). Since 𝑉𝑉 † ⪯ Π𝜋 ⊗ 𝐼 𝑘 , we have

                                  𝐾 ∗ (𝐴)† 𝐾 ∗ (𝐴) = 𝑉 † (𝐴† ⊗ 𝐼 𝑘 )𝑉𝑉 † (𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼 𝑘 )𝑉
                                                  ⪯ 𝑉 † (𝐴† 𝐴 ⊗ 𝐼 𝑘 )𝑉
                                                  = 𝐾 ∗ (𝐴† 𝐴)
                                                  ⪯ 𝐾 ∗ (∥𝐴∥2∞ Π𝜋 )
                                                  ⪯ ∥𝐴∥2∞ 𝐾 ∗ (Π𝜋 )
                                                  = ∥𝐴∥2∞ Π𝜋 ,



                                                         66
where the last two inequalities use the positivity and unitality of 𝐾 ∗ .27 Taking spectral norms thus
gives us ∥𝐾 ∗ (𝐴)∥∞ ⩽ ∥𝐴∥∞ . Hence, if 𝐾 ∗ (𝐴) = 𝜇𝐴 with 𝐴 ≠ 0, then |𝜇| ⩽ 1. Since the eigenvalues of
𝐾 are simply conjugates of those of 𝐾 ∗ , the preceding spectral bound then gives us 𝜆 𝑗 ⩽ 1. Thus,
every eigenvalue of 𝐾 lies in [0, 1].

Remark A.2 (Alternative proof via Rényi divergence). In the classical case, Lemma 10 admits a
more natural proof by recasting 𝑝s (ℰ) as a conditional collision entropy and invoking Jensen’s
inequality. Such a proof does extend to the quantum setting via the data processing inequality for the
sandwiched order-2 quantum Rényi divergence [MLDS+ 13, WWY14], established independently
by Frank and Lieb [FL13] and by Beigi [Bei13]. For completeness and greater self-containment, we
presented this alternative proof via the Petz map. In this formulation, the Petz–Gram inequality in
Fact 32(iii) serves as the noncommutative analog of the conditional-Jensen step.                     ⊳

As 𝑛 → ∞, the left-hand side becomes 𝐶(Φ𝜎 ) by Definition 2.6. This completes the proof.                                 □

A.4    Theorem 7: The cq channel coding strong converse
In this subsection, we prove the cq channel coding strong converse.28 To facilitate the exposition,
we recall for the reader the proof for the classical BSC case. Fix 𝑝 ∈ (0, 12 ), and suppose that
{𝒟𝑐 : 𝑐 ∈ 𝒞 } are the decoding regions of a deterministic decoder for BSC(𝑝). If c is sent and the
noise is z ∈ {0, 1}𝑛 , then the decoder succeeds exactly when c ⊕ z ∈ 𝒟𝑐 . Now, define the function
                                                       1
                                        𝑡 𝑛 (𝑧) B          |{𝑐 ∈ 𝒞 : 𝑐 ⊕ 𝑧 ∈ 𝒟𝑐 }| .

refinements [Str64, Ari73].

Thus, whenever 𝑅(𝒞 ) > 1 − ℎ(𝑝) by a fixed amount, choosing 𝜀 smaller than that gap makes both

    The cq channel proof repeats these three steps. Output symmetry plays a similar role as
XOR in the BSC case, producing a matrix 𝑇𝑛 that plays the role of 𝑡 𝑛 . Its expectations under 𝜎0⊗𝑛
and 𝜎¯ ⊗𝑛 reproduce the two identities above. Finally, two spectral cutoffs replace the BSC typical
set—one cutoff to say that the probability of z (resp. 𝜎0⊗𝑛 ) is not too large, and another to say that
the probability of y (resp. 𝜎¯ ⊗𝑛 ) is not too small—and provide the required matrix version of the
likelihood ratio bound.

Proof of Theorem 7. Recall that each decoder matrix 𝐷𝑐 is designed to recognize its corresponding

      The first cutoff removes eigenvalues of 𝜎0⊗𝑛 that are atypically large, so as to make the bulk of its

− Tr(𝜎0 log 𝜎)

A.8    Proposition 14: Qubit entropy in Pauli coordinates
In this subsection, we prove Proposition 14.

Proof of Proposition 14. Since 𝑋 2 = 𝑍 2 = 𝐼 and 𝑋𝑍 = −𝑍𝑋, we see that

                                             (𝑥𝑋 + 𝑧𝑍)2 = (𝑥 2 + 𝑧 2 )𝐼.

Since the
        √ Hermitian√ matrix 𝑥𝑋 + 𝑧𝑍 is traceless, we therefore see that the eigenvalues of√𝑥𝑋 + 𝑧𝑍
are {− 𝑥 + 𝑧 , + 𝑥 2 + 𝑧 2 }. Therefore, the two eigenvalues of 12 (𝐼 + 𝑥𝑋 + 𝑧𝑍) are 12 (1 − 𝑥 2 + 𝑧 2 )

and 12 (1 + 𝑥 2 + 𝑧 2 ). Substituting them into the definition of von Neumann entropy thus gives

A.9    Propositions 15 and 16: Masking
In this subsection, we prove Propositions 15 and 16.

Proof of Proposition 15. First, observe that the average state is Σ̄ B 12 (Σ0 + Σ1 ) = 12 𝜏¯ 𝛼 ⊕ 12 𝜏¯ 𝛼 . Let
                                              −1/2        −1/2                          −1/2        −1/2
                            𝑁0 B (1 − 𝛼)𝜏¯ 𝛼         𝜏0 𝜏¯ 𝛼     ,             𝑁1 B 𝛼 𝜏¯ 𝛼     𝜏1 𝜏¯ 𝛼     ,

be the PGM observables of the non-uniform binary ensemble {(1 − 𝛼, 𝜏0 ), (𝛼, 𝜏1 )}. Since Σ̄−1/2 =
√ −1/2       −1/2
 2(𝜏¯ 𝛼 ⊕ 𝜏¯ 𝛼 ) on its support, the PGM definition gives

                                       𝑀0 = 𝑁0 ⊕ 𝑁1 ,                 𝑀1 = 𝑁1 ⊕ 𝑁0 .

Thus, reading the block first leaves the non-uniform PGM in either block, with the outcome labels
reversed in the second. Its error is
                              1              1
               𝑝 e (Σ0 , Σ1 ) = Tr(Σ0 𝑀1 ) + Tr(Σ1 𝑀0 )
                              2              2
                              1−𝛼                 𝛼             𝛼          1−𝛼
                            =       Tr(𝜏0 𝑁1 ) + Tr(𝜏1 𝑁0 ) + Tr(𝜏1 𝑁0 ) +     Tr(𝜏0 𝑁1 )
                                 2                2             2           2
                            = (1 − 𝛼) Tr(𝜏0 𝑁1 ) + 𝛼 Tr(𝜏1 𝑁0 )
                                                 −1/2          −1/2                                −1/2        −1/2
                            = 𝛼(1 − 𝛼) Tr(𝜏0 𝜏¯ 𝛼        𝜏1 𝜏¯ 𝛼      ) + 𝛼(1 − 𝛼) Tr(𝜏1 𝜏¯ 𝛼             𝜏0 𝜏¯ 𝛼     )
                                                     −1/2          −1/2
                            = 2𝛼(1 − 𝛼) Tr(𝜏0 𝜏¯ 𝛼          𝜏1 𝜏¯ 𝛼       ),

where cyclicity of the trace gives the last equality. This is also the PGM error of {(1 − 𝛼, 𝜏0 ), (𝛼, 𝜏1 )},
proving (22).                                                                                              □

Proof of Proposition 16. If the eigenvalues of 𝜏0 and 𝜏1 are {𝜆 𝑖 }𝑖 and {𝜇 𝑗 } 𝑗 , respectively, then the
nonzero eigenvalues of Σ0 are {(1 − 𝛼)𝜆 𝑖 }𝑖 and {𝛼𝜇 𝑗 } 𝑗 . Therefore

                                  𝑆(Σ0 ) = 𝑆(Σ1 ) = ℎ(𝛼) + (1 − 𝛼)𝑆(𝜏0 ) + 𝛼𝑆(𝜏1 ).

Likewise, the two copies of 𝜏¯ 𝛼 in Σ̄ = 12 𝜏¯ 𝛼 ⊕ 21 𝜏¯ 𝛼 give 𝑆(Σ̄) = 1 + 𝑆(𝜏¯ 𝛼 ). Substitution in the Holevo
identity proves (23).                                                                                          □
